## Results

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Results")\]

### National results

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: National results")\]

Results listed below correspond to the groupings created by the [Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Interior_(France) "Minister of the Interior (France)"), which may differ slightly from the figures reported in other sources due to reclassification of candidates into different political parties and alliances. Differences noted in the footnotes of the national results table below reflect political parties and alliances attributed to candidates by _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_. The Ministry of the Interior classifications generally include fewer candidates within the groupings for [Ensemble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensemble_(political_coalition) "Ensemble (political coalition)"), the [New Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Popular_Front "New Popular Front"), and [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") when compared with _Le Monde_ and other media outlets which opt to not use the Ministry of the Interior's candidate classifications; as a result, the official vote and seat totals listed below may be lower than the ones reported in those sources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-6)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-2)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)

On the basis of the final results published by the Ministry of the Interior and candidate classifications of _Le Monde_, the New Popular Front obtained 182 seats, compared to Ensemble with 168, RN-supported candidates with 143, and 45 for LR (compared with 56 claimed by the party, due to differences in candidate self-identification). Out of those 182 NFP deputies, 74 are attributed to LFI, 59 to the PS, 28 to LE, 9 to the PCF, 5 to Génération.s, 5 to miscellaneous left deputies, and 2 to regionalists. _Le Monde_ also tallied 102 Renaissance, 33 MoDem, 26 Horizons, 3 miscellaneous centre, and 2 UDI deputies, as well as 1 deputy each under the labels of miscellaneous right and [Agir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agir_(France) "Agir (France)"). The Ministry of the Interior does not classify candidates by party but rather by "political nuances" as defined within the [National directory of elected officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_directory_of_elected_officials "National directory of elected officials"), so no official breakdown of the results of NFP or Ensemble candidates by self-declared party is available.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-7)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-3)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024) Although nuances for several components of those alliances were provided in 2024, neither are fully compatibilised with the actual or self-declared affiliation of candidates within these alliances.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NFPtotal-5)[\[b\]](#refnote-NFPtotal)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Etotal-5)[\[c\]](#refnote-Etotal)

Second round configurations

(before candidate withdrawals)[&#8202;](#ref-back-quadrangulaires-3)[\[6\]](#refnote-quadrangulaires)

Turnout in the first round of the legislative elections was exceptionally high, including 2.7 million [proxy voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_voting "Proxy voting") requests, with pollsters estimating the final turnout to be the highest for the first round of a legislative election since [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_French_legislative_election "1997 French legislative election"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ipsos1997-1)[\[5\]](#refnote-Ipsos1997) which was confirmed in the provisional results with turnout at 66.71%. In the first round, the RN and their allies secured the largest share of the vote in the first round with 33.21% of the vote, followed by the parties of the New Popular Front with 28.21%,[&#8202;](#ref-back-NFPtotal-10)[\[b\]](#refnote-NFPtotal) those of Ensemble with 21.28%,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Etotal-10)[\[c\]](#refnote-Etotal) and LR candidates with 6.57%, with an overall turnout of 66.71%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-5)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)

On the basis of the first round results (not accounting for candidate withdrawals after the first round), 306 constituencies were headed to three-way runoffs and 5 to four-way runoffs,[&#8202;](#ref-back-quadrangulaires-4)[\[6\]](#refnote-quadrangulaires) with only 89 three-way and 2 four-way runoffs remaining after candidate withdrawals announced ahead of the registration deadline for the second round.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMwithdrawals-2)[\[7\]](#refnote-LMwithdrawals) A total of 76 candidates were directly elected in the first round,[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-6)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024) and RN-supported candidates qualified for the second round in 444 other constituencies, compared to 415 for the NFP, 321 for Ensemble, and 63 for LR (according to _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")'_ s classifications of candidates by political affiliation).[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMresults-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-LMresults)

### Voting instructions and withdrawals

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Voting instructions and withdrawals")\]

#### Before the first round

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Before the first round")\]

In an interview with [Cyril Hanouna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_Hanouna "Cyril Hanouna") on [Europe 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_1 "Europe 1") on 24 June, [Gender Equality Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Gender_Equality,_Diversity_and_Equal_Opportunities "Minister for Gender Equality, Diversity and Equal Opportunities") [Aurore Bergé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurore_Berg%C3%A9 "Aurore Bergé"), like [Emmanuel Macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Macron "Emmanuel Macron"), declined to specify second-round voting instructions between either the [New Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Popular_Front "New Popular Front") (NFP) and [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally") (RN) while also implying that any such discussions would not take place until after the first round.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-271)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berge-1)[\[113\]](#refnote-Berge) Both of [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_(French_political_party) "Renaissance (French political party)")'s alliance partners' leaders, [François Bayrou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Bayrou "François Bayrou") of the [Democratic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Movement_(France) "Democratic Movement (France)") (MoDem) and [Édouard Philippe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Philippe "Édouard Philippe") of [Horizons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizons_(political_party) "Horizons (political party)"), also refused to address the issue, with allies of Macron reportedly divided on this question.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-272)

On the afternoon of 25 June, Macron and the leaders of Renaissance, MoDem, Horizons, the [Union of Democrats and Independents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democrats_and_Independents "Union of Democrats and Independents") (UDI), the [Radical Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Party_(France) "Radical Party (France)"), and several other members of his coterie met to discuss the matter, with a general consensus emerging among participants to call to block the RN and [La France Insoumise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_France_Insoumise "La France Insoumise") (LFI) candidates in the second round and potentially withdraw on a case-by-case basis, though no decision was reached by the end of the meeting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LP0625-1)[\[97\]](#refnote-LP0625) Interviewed on the evening of 26 June, Bayrou remarked, "we will vote for neither a National Rally nor LFI candidate ... why have the political forces, which I consider to be republicans, gone and settled themselves under the yoke of a very radical, brutal, violent far-left?"[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pradie-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-Pradie) Philippe then called on all third-placed Horizons candidates who advanced to give up their candidacy in the second round.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive-1)[\[8\]](#refnote-LMlive)

[Marine Tondelier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Tondelier "Marine Tondelier"), leader of [The Ecologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ecologists_(political_party) "The Ecologists (political party)"), announced in an interview the same day that candidates from her party would withdraw if they were eligible to advance to the second round but finished in third place,[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-274) a view also shared by [Raphaël Glucksmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapha%C3%ABl_Glucksmann "Raphaël Glucksmann") of [Place Publique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Publique "Place Publique").[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-275) LFI [MEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_European_Parliament "Member of European Parliament") [Manon Aubry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manon_Aubry "Manon Aubry") stated that the decisions of LFI candidates in three-way races would be made on a case-by-case basis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-276) [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_(France) "Socialist Party (France)") leader [Olivier Faure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Faure "Olivier Faure"), along with Glucksmann, Tondelier, and two government ministers ( [Clément Beaune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cl%C3%A9ment_Beaune "Clément Beaune") and [Agnès Pannier-Runacher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agn%C3%A8s_Pannier-Runacher "Agnès Pannier-Runacher")) signed onto an open letter published in _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_ on 25 June pushing for all parties facing to reach an agreement to withdraw candidates in order to block the RN, though no LFI representatives signed onto the letter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-277) On 26 June, [Jean-Luc Mélenchon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Luc_M%C3%A9lenchon "Jean-Luc Mélenchon") indicated that LFI would issue voting instructions after the first round and told his supporters to not vote for the RN.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LPFaure-1)[\[94\]](#refnote-LPFaure) In a press release on 27 June, the [French Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Communist_Party "French Communist Party") (PCF) confirmed that it would instruct its voters to support a "republican candidate" against the RN in the second round, and that its candidates would withdraw if they advanced to the second round but finished third in the first.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LFPCF-1)[\[181\]](#refnote-LFPCF)

While he was opposed to the prospect of an alliance between [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") (LR) and the National Rally, LR vice president [François-Xavier Bellamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois-Xavier_Bellamy "François-Xavier Bellamy") declared that he would support candidates of the RN against those of the NFP in the second round, even in the case that they were not LFI candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-278) In an interview on 26 June, [Julien Aubert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julien_Aubert "Julien Aubert") declared that he would vote for any candidate against the RN in most cases, but would vote for them if faced with a LFI candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pradie-1)[\[237\]](#refnote-Pradie)

#### After the first round

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: After the first round")\]

| Parties (including allies, as classified by _Le Monde_) |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NFP–ENS–LR–RN, no withdrawals |  | 2 |
| NFP–ENS–LR–RN, ENS withdrawal |  | 2 |
| NFP–ENS–RN–other, NFP/other withdrawals |  | 1 |
| NFP–ENS–RN, no withdrawals |  | 70 |
| NFP–LR–RN, no withdrawals |  | 13 |
| Other three-way races with no withdrawals |  | 4 |
| NFP–ENS–RN, NFP withdrawal |  | 99 |
| NFP–ENS–RN, ENS withdrawal |  | 79 |
| NFP–LR–RN, NFP withdrawal |  | 27 |
| Other three-way races with one withdrawal |  | 14 |

Second round withdrawals by configuration of qualified candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-7)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMwithdrawals-3)[\[7\]](#refnote-LMwithdrawals)

In a press release after the first round, Ensemble announced that they would call upon third-placed candidates to withdraw in constituencies where other candidates "with shared values" were able to beat the RN, including the possibility of desisting in favour of selected LFI candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive-2)[\[8\]](#refnote-LMlive) Bergé, interviewed after the first round, reaffirmed that candidates would withdraw to block the RN except in the case that it might help to elect an LFI deputy, with other parties in the alliance sharing a similar line of case-by-case withdrawals in runoffs with LFI candidates,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive2-1)[\[160\]](#refnote-LMlive2) but Attal also emphasised that withdrawals did not constitute endorsements and that the alliance would not officially issue any voting instructions, even if individual candidates did.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive3-4)[\[151\]](#refnote-LMlive3)

A story published by _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_ on 5 July suggested that Attal spearheaded the anti-RN blockade to Macron's chagrin, with the latter annoyed by Attal's relative independence. Macron also called Ensemble candidates to pressure them to not drop out up until the last moment, with his inner circle reportedly becoming more comfortable with the idea of an RN victory even as Attal warned about the dangers of the far-right coming to power.[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-279)

Mélenchon ultimately announced that all third-placed candidates of the New Popular Front who advanced to the second round in constituencies where the RN placed first would stand down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive-3)[\[8\]](#refnote-LMlive) In duels between left-of-centre candidates, the PS called for voters to support the higher-placed candidate, as well as for the withdrawal of lower-placed candidates,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive3-5)[\[151\]](#refnote-LMlive3) a view also echoed by the leadership of the [French Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Communist_Party "French Communist Party").[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-280)

The Republicans ultimately chose not to give any voting instructions for the second round.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive-4)[\[8\]](#refnote-LMlive)

According to RN deputy [Sébastien Chenu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9bastien_Chenu "Sébastien Chenu"), a number of deputies, including those belonging to Ensemble, reached out to ask third-placed RN candidates to withdraw in order to block candidates of the left from winning in the second round, a request which Chenu indicated the party's leadership would consider.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive2-2)[\[160\]](#refnote-LMlive2)

According to the classifications of _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_, 89 three-way and 2 four-way runoffs remained after the publicly announced withdrawals of 134 NFP-supported and 82 Ensemble-supported candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMwithdrawals-4)[\[7\]](#refnote-LMwithdrawals) All but 5 NFP candidates withdrew in races where they placed 3rd or below in the first round and RN-backed candidates also qualified, compared to 15 Ensemble candidates (of which 9 were in races where LFI qualified) and 12 [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") or [miscellaneous right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscellaneous_right "Miscellaneous right") candidates (of which 5 were in races where LFI qualified) in the same situation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-8)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMwithdrawals-5)[\[7\]](#refnote-LMwithdrawals) Two candidates who also initially registered for the second round – Dominique Despras in [Rhône's 8th constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne's_8th_constituency "Rhône's 8th constituency") and France Moreau in [Maine-et-Loire's 5th constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine-et-Loire's_5th_constituency "Maine-et-Loire's 5th constituency") – changed their mind after finding out about other candidates' registrations in the second round. Because they were unable to officially withdraw at this point, they declined to submit any of their paper ballots, meaning that voters did not find paper ballots with the name of either candidate at polling stations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-281)

### Second round

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Second round")\]

Provisional results placed turnout in the second round at 66.63%, the highest level since [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_French_legislative_election "1997 French legislative election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-282)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-9)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)

Contrary to pre-election projections, NFP-supported candidates won the largest number of seats after the second round, with Ensemble candidates also beating expectations by coming second ahead of RN-supported candidates in third, trailed by LR candidates in fourth place. According to candidate labeling by the [Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Interior_(France) "Minister of the Interior (France)"), candidates belonging to NFP parties received 180 seats (well short of the 289 needed for a majority),[&#8202;](#ref-back-NFPtotal-11)[\[b\]](#refnote-NFPtotal) compared to 159 for those belonging to Ensemble parties,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Etotal-11)[\[c\]](#refnote-Etotal) 142 for RN-supported candidates, and 39 for LR candidates, resulting in no party controlling a parliamentary majority. Unofficial media classifications of candidates' affiliations may differ slightly from those used by the Ministry of Interior: according to _Le Monde'_ s analysis, 182 NFP-affiliated candidates were elected, compared with 168 for Ensemble, 143 for the RN, and 45 for LR,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-10)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024) with 80 constituencies shifting rightwards and 75 shifting leftwards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) The PS more than doubled its number of elected deputies compared to 2022 and the RN gained the second-most seats, while Renaissance suffered the most significant losses of any individual party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-284)[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-285)

An Ipsos survey suggested that the " [republican front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Front_(French_Fifth_Republic) "Republican Front (French Fifth Republic)")" against the RN remained fairly strong in the second round, with first-round NFP voters supporting Ensemble candidates over RN ones by a 72%–3% margin in second-round duels and first-round Ensemble voters supporting PS/LE/PCF candidates over RN ones by a 54%–15% margin in second-round duels. NFP voters also backed LR candidates over RN ones in second-round duels by a 70%–2% margin, and Ensemble voters by a 79%–4% margin. Even in the case of LFI–RN duels, first-round Ensemble voters supported LFI candidates over RN ones by a 43%–19% margin.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-286)

The number of women elected to the National Assembly declined for the second election in a row, from 215 in 2022 to 208 (36%), with the NFP having the highest share at 41% followed by Ensemble with 39%, RN-supported deputies with 32%, and LR with 27%. The average age of the National Assembly remained similar at 49 years and 2 months, with the RN having both the youngest ( [Flavien Termet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flavien_Termet "Flavien Termet"), 22) and oldest ( [José Gonzalez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Gonzalez_(French_politician) "José Gonzalez (French politician)"), 81) deputies. The average age of the RN-supported and NFP deputies was 47, compared to 51 for Ensemble and 52 for LR. Out of the 577 elected deputies, 408 were re-elected, with the highest rate of re-election for Ensemble and LR deputies (both roughly 84.5%) followed by NFP (63%) and RN-supported (57%) deputies. The managerial and professional class is overwhelmingly overrepresented among these 577 deputies, accounting for more than 60% of all new elected officials, with this percentage being similar among all four of the largest groupings. Only three blue-collar workers were elected (0.5% of the National Assembly), compared to their share of 19% in the overall population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-287)

For the first time in their history, the RN managed to elect candidates in [overseas constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_constituencies_of_the_National_Assembly_of_France#Overseas:_27_constituencies "List of constituencies of the National Assembly of France"):[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-:12) [Anchya Bamana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchya_Bamana "Anchya Bamana"), contesting in [Mayotte's 2nd constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayotte's_2nd_constituency "Mayotte's 2nd constituency"), and [Joseph Rivière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Rivi%C3%A8re_(politician) "Joseph Rivière (politician)"), running in [Réunion's 3rd constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9union's_3rd_constituency "Réunion's 3rd constituency"), both won a seat in the National Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-1)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02)

[Emmanuel Tjibaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Tjibaou "Emmanuel Tjibaou"), the son of late [Kanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanak_people "Kanak people") independence leader [Jean-Marie Tjibaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Tjibaou "Jean-Marie Tjibaou"), was elected to the National Assembly for [New Caledonia's 2nd constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Caledonia's_2nd_constituency "New Caledonia's 2nd constituency"):[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-:1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-291) in the process, he became the first [pro-independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-independence_movement "Pro-independence movement") candidate to win a seat since 1986, and his victory was seen as a setback for the French loyalist movement in [New Caledonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Caledonia "New Caledonia"), in the aftermath of the [civil unrest across the archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_New_Caledonia_unrest "2024 New Caledonia unrest") earlier in the year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[254\]](#refnote-:1) [Peio Dufau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peio_Dufau "Peio Dufau") became the first left-wing _[abertzale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abertzale "Abertzale")_ candidate to get elected to the National Assembly, having run under the banner of [Euskal Herria Bai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euskal_Herria_Bai "Euskal Herria Bai") (EH Bai), supported by the NFP, in [Pyrénées-Atlantiques's 6th constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyr%C3%A9n%C3%A9es-Atlantiques's_6th_constituency "Pyrénées-Atlantiques's 6th constituency").[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-292)

## Aftermath

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Aftermath")\]

See also: [2024–2025 French political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_French_political_crisis "2024–2025 French political crisis")

In his year-end address on 31 December 2024, Macron acknowledged that calling the election had "brought more division in the (National) Assembly than solutions for the French" and added that "I take my full part for that".[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-296)

### Potential outcomes

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Potential outcomes")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Place_de_la_R%C3%A9publique%2C_Juillet_7_2024_06.jpg/250px-Place_de_la_R%C3%A9publique%2C_Juillet_7_2024_06.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Place_de_la_R%C3%A9publique,_Juillet_7_2024_06.jpg) Celebrations on the [Place de la République](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_R%C3%A9publique "Place de la République") in Paris on 7 July

Although the [president of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_France "President of France") theoretically has the power to appoint any individual as [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_France "Prime Minister of France"), the likelihood of a [motion of no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence "Motion of no confidence") means that in practice, tradition dictates that the president must nominate someone from any political bloc with an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority "Absolute majority") of seats in the incoming [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_(France) "National Assembly (France)"), resulting in [cohabitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohabitation_(government)#France "Cohabitation (government)") in the event of an opposition victory. When no political force obtains an absolute majority of seats, any government will face the constant threat of a motion of no confidence and handle legislation on a case-by-case basis unless a coalition is secured to avoid this threat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LEMazuir-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-LEMazuir)

Due to the tripolarisation of the electorate, unprecedented institutional deadlock is a significant possibility if no bloc proves able to secure a majority of votes in the National Assembly to insulate themselves from a motion of no confidence. [Snap elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snap_election "Snap election") can only be called at least a year after the previous legislative election under the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_France "Constitution of France").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LEMazuir-1)[\[260\]](#refnote-LEMazuir) Some analysts also envisaged the possibility of an [Italian-style technocratic government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technocratic_government_(Italy) "Technocratic government (Italy)") or a [2020 Belgian-like temporary minority government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Belgian_government_formation#Formateur_Sophie_Wilm%C3%A8s_(16_March_2020_%E2%80%93_17_March_2020) "2019–2020 Belgian government formation") of independent figures prior to a second snap election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-298)

### Post-election manoeuvring

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Post-election manoeuvring")\]

Attal announced that he would resign as prime minister just after the results were announced. Macron was not required to accept Attal's resignation, and the latter noted that he would be willing to remain in his post as long as necessary,[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-299) and Macron ultimately refused his resignation "for the time being" on the morning of 8 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-1)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) In a letter published in the regional press on 10 July, Macron declared that "nobody won" the elections and added that the current government would remain in place until it was clear that a new "necessarily plural" government of "political forces that recognise themselves in republican institutions" could be formed,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) with leading figures on the left decrying the letter as a "refus\[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[al\] to recognise the result of the elections" showing that Macron was "in denial" and attempting a "democratic coup" by signaling that he would not allow the NFP to lead the next government if it included LFI members.[\[264\]](#refnote-301) Macron accepted his resignation on 16 July,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LPlive-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-LPlive) allowing ministers to vote for the president of the National Assembly while remaining in place as a caretaker government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive14-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-LMlive14)

In an interview on 1 July, [Aurore Bergé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurore_Berg%C3%A9 "Aurore Bergé") signaled openness to a coalition with members of other parties, including "the Republicans who didn't want to corrupt themselves with Éric Ciotti and with the RN, with certain members of the Socialist Party, the ecologists, the communists,"[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive2-3)[\[160\]](#refnote-LMlive2) a view shared by the leader of alliance partner [Horizons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizons_(political_party) "Horizons (political party)") [Édouard Philippe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Philippe "Édouard Philippe").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LFlive-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-LFlive) Philippe called for an "agreement which will stabilise the political situation" excluding LFI and the RN. Séjourné likewise excluded the possibility of governing alongside Mélenchon and some of his allies on the left, saying that there would be certain prerequisites for any potential coalition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-3)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6) Interviewed on 8 July, Darmanin envisaged the possibility of cooperation with the PS on some issues where they differed from LFI,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-2)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) but went on to add that he was opposed to working with ecologist deputies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-1)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) On 9 July, several Renaissance deputies threatened to file a motion of no confidence in the event that the next government included LFI deputies, and in the face of these threats and Attal's continuation as prime minister, the NFP warned Macron "against any attempt to hijack the institutions."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8)

A number of key figures indicated their preference for working with the right rather than the left, with Philippe arguing for a "technical agreement" but not coalition with LR in addition to Darmanin publicly stating that "the country is on the right\[, so\] we must govern on the right," and Macron reportedly met with LR Senate president [Gérard Larcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Larcher "Gérard Larcher") on the evening of 8 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-1)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-306) Bergé and Darmanin both publicly appealed for the support of LR deputies the following day, with the latter also confirming that he would also vote for a motion of no confidence against a NFP government, but Bayrou was less receptive to the idea, suggesting that he preferred a coalition which still involved some figures from the left.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-2)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9)

On 2 July, LFI national coordinator [Manuel Bompard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Bompard "Manuel Bompard") announced that his party would not participate in a [grand coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_coalition "Grand coalition") including LFI and Ensemble,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LFlive-1)[\[267\]](#refnote-LFlive) and Attal likewise ruled out the possibility of a coalition with LFI.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive3-6)[\[151\]](#refnote-LMlive3) Tondelier declined to rule out the possibility of participating in a coalition but indicated her desire not to do so and her opposition to a Macron-aligned prime minister.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-307)[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-308) Just after the second round, Mélenchon called for the appointment of a prime minister from the left, echoed by Tondelier and Faure, who said that the NFP intended to govern and would not support any Ensemble-led coalition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-4)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6) [Yannick Jadot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannick_Jadot "Yannick Jadot") announced that the NFP would propose its government the week of the second round, Faure declared that the alliance would agree upon a name for prime minister by then, Bompard said that the NFP would implement its programme in its entirety,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-3)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) and [Sandrine Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandrine_Rousseau "Sandrine Rousseau") pledged not to repeatedly invoke [article 49.3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_49_of_the_French_Constitution#Commitment_of_responsibility_on_a_bill_(49.3) "Article 49 of the French Constitution") unlike recent governments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-309)

PS alliance negotiator [Johanna Rolland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johanna_Rolland "Johanna Rolland"), along with [Carole Delga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carole_Delga "Carole Delga"), welcomed the potential support of the leftmost Ensemble deputies in a NFP-led coalition,[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-310)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-3)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) while Faure resisted pressure from Macron allies to break away from LFI in order to form a government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-311) [Huguette Bello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huguette_Bello "Huguette Bello") emerged as a possible name for prime minister on 12 July, proposed by Roussel but resisted by the PS, which failed to approve Bello's candidacy on 13 July and called for further discussions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive12-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-LMlive12) LFI reacted by suspending negotiations, demanding that all parties agree upon on a single candidacy for the presidency of the National Assembly, and refusing the possibility of a civil society figure being appointed prime minister, after which the PS, LE, and PCF pushed for discussions to resume and proposed appointing a civil society figure as prime minister,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive13-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-LMlive13) [Laurence Tubiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurence_Tubiana "Laurence Tubiana"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-314) a hypothesis publicly rejected by LFI figureheads as too "Macron-compatible."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LPlive-1)[\[265\]](#refnote-LPlive) Tubiana dismissed the possibility of becoming prime minister on 22 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-315) On 23 July, the NFP announced that it would propose 37-year-old civil servant [Lucie Castets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucie_Castets "Lucie Castets"), director of finance and purchasing for the city of Paris, for the post of prime minister, though Macron will not appoint a new government until mid-August at earliest and noted that the question was not of the name for prime minister but rather the ability to find a majority to govern.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive15-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-LMlive15)

[Laurent Wauquiez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurent_Wauquiez "Laurent Wauquiez") ruled out the possibility of LR participating in a potential coalition just after the results were announced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-5)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6) President of the [LR group in the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Republicans_(France) "Senate Republicans (France)") [Bruno Retailleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Retailleau "Bruno Retailleau") ruled out the possibility of working with any coalition of the left, and like others within his party, deemed the LR brand to be "dead."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-4)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) Despite these statements, some LR figures still called for a pro-Macron alliance in the National Assembly, including [Xavier Bertrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Bertrand "Xavier Bertrand") and [Olivier Marleix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Marleix "Olivier Marleix").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-2)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8) After being elected president of the group, Wauquiez proclaimed that he would not agree to a coalition but would be open to a "legislative pact," and also signaled that the right would vote for a [motion of no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence "Motion of no confidence") if any future government included LFI members,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-4)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) a position also held by Larcher.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive10-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-LMlive10)

After falling short of pre-election expectations, Le Pen said that the RN's victory "is only postponed" and that she could not be disappointed with the party's gains in the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-6)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6) On 8 July, [Sébastien Chenu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9bastien_Chenu "Sébastien Chenu") appeared to express regret about the controversies surrounding many of the candidates who went on to lose their races, and affirmed that the RN would remain in opposition "without compromise,"[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-5)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) and [Zoltán Kovács](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Kov%C3%A1cs_(politician,_born_1969) "Zoltán Kovács (politician, born 1969)") announced that Bardella would chair the [Patriots for Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriots_for_Europe "Patriots for Europe") group in the [European Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Parliament "European Parliament").[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-318) Gilles Pennelle, the general director of the party who oversaw the selection of RN candidates, resigned the day after the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-6)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) On 11 July, [Renaud Labaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaud_Labaye?action=edit&redlink=1 "Renaud Labaye (page does not exist)") initially announced that the RN would not automatically vote for a motion of no confidence in a NFP-led government including LFI members, but rather consider legislative texts on a case-by-case basis,[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-319) but Le Pen subsequently clarified that the RN would do so in the case that any government contained LFI or LE ministers, regardless of who led it,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive10-1)[\[280\]](#refnote-LMlive10) and Bardella confirmed that they would immediately vote for a motion of no confidence in any NFP minority government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-320)

### Prime Ministerial appointment

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Prime Ministerial appointment")\]

#### Appointment background

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Appointment background")\]

After the dissolution of the [16th legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_legislature_of_the_French_Fifth_Republic "16th legislature of the French Fifth Republic") by Macron on 9 June 2024, the [early legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_French_legislative_elections "2024 French legislative elections") took place on 30 June and 7 July. While the [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally") was originally anticipated to obtain a relative majority, it came third behind the [New Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Popular_Front "New Popular Front") and [Ensemble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensemble_(political_coalition) "Ensemble (political coalition)").

Then-Prime Minister [Gabriel Attal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Attal "Gabriel Attal"), having only served six months in office, presented his resignation to Macron, who accepted it on 16 July. The day before the opening of the [Paris Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Summer_Olympic_Games "2024 Summer Olympic Games"), the New Popular Front proposed that [Lucie Castets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucie_Castets "Lucie Castets") be nominated as prime minister. In reaction, arguing that "no one won \[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[the elections\]", Macron announced the holding of consultations to form a government.[\[284\]](#refnote-321)

On 16 August, at the end of the Olympic Games, Macron invited party leaders and presidents of parliamentary groups from both chambers to the [Palais de l'Élysée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_de_l'%C3%89lys%C3%A9e "Palais de l'Élysée") on 23 August to try to form a government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-322) After the President received the party leaders and the presidents of parliamentary groups, Macron's office announced in a press release on 26 August that Castets would not be appointed prime minister.[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-323)

On 2 September, Emmanuel Macron received nominations from [Bernard Cazeneuve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Cazeneuve "Bernard Cazeneuve"), the former [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_(France) "Socialist Party (France)") Prime Minister from 2016 to 2017, and [Xavier Bertrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Bertrand "Xavier Bertrand"), a former [Hauts-de-France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauts-de-France "Hauts-de-France") region president.[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-324) Faced with the risk of a motion of censure, the candidacy of [Michel Barnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Barnier "Michel Barnier") was considered on 4 September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-325)

#### Final appointment

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Final appointment")\]

Main article: [Barnier government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnier_government "Barnier government")

On 5 September 2024, [Michel Barnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Barnier "Michel Barnier") was appointed as prime minister by Macron, heading a Parliament divided nearly evenly between the leftist New Popular Front, which holds the plurality of seats, Macron's centrist to centre-right Ensemble, and the far-right National Rally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-326)[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-327)

While the NFP has announced it would move a [motion of no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence "Motion of no confidence") against any government not led by them, the [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally") announces that it will wait for the general policy statement of the new government before deciding whether to support any motions of no confidence or censure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-328)

### Composition of the National Assembly

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Composition of the National Assembly")\]

Plans for governance were likely to only be solidified after the formation of [parliamentary groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_group "Parliamentary group") in the [17th legislature of the French Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_legislature_of_the_French_Fifth_Republic "17th legislature of the French Fifth Republic") clarified the alignment of elected deputies starting on 11 July, which was followed by the election of the next [president of the National Assembly of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_National_Assembly_of_France "President of the National Assembly of France") on 18 July as well as the appointment of vice presidents, quaestors, and secretaries as part of the [bureau of the National Assembly of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_the_National_Assembly_of_France?action=edit&redlink=1 "Bureau of the National Assembly of France (page does not exist)")\[ [fr](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau%20de%20l'Assembl%C3%A9e%20nationale%20fran%C3%A7aise "fr:Bureau de l'Assemblée nationale française")\].[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-329) Macron is expected to allow the bloc which is able to elect the next president of the National Assembly to form the next government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-330) Numerous figures on the left also called upon deputies to block the RN from obtaining key posts in the National Assembly,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-5)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) with pro-Macron deputies potentially also willing to do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-331) Renaissance deputy [Yaël Braun-Pivet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya%C3%ABl_Braun-Pivet "Yaël Braun-Pivet") was re-elected president of the National Assembly with 220 votes in the third round of voting on 18 July owing to the support of LR deputies, ahead of NFP-supported PCF deputy [André Chassaigne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Chassaigne "André Chassaigne") with 207.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive14-1)[\[266\]](#refnote-LMlive14) Across 19 and 20 July, the NFP obtained 12 of 22 posts in the bureau of the National Assembly, with the RN shut out of all posts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-332)

Shortly after the elections, [Clémentine Autain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cl%C3%A9mentine_Autain "Clémentine Autain"), like Ruffin, announced that she would not join the [La France Insoumise group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_France_Insoumise_group "La France Insoumise group") in the incoming legislature, instead hoping to form a new group,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-7)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) potentially alongside [Christophe Bex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christophe_Bex "Christophe Bex") and LFI incumbents who managed to win re-election against official LFI candidates including [Alexis Corbière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Corbi%C3%A8re "Alexis Corbière"), [Hendrik Davi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_Davi "Hendrik Davi"), and [Danielle Simonnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danielle_Simonnet "Danielle Simonnet"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-333) with the five of them seeking to form a new group with members of the PCF, LE, and [Génération.s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9n%C3%A9ration.s "Génération.s") and Corbière also expressing his willingness to join a unified NFP group,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-3)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8) and all but Ruffin announcing the creation of the political movement [L'Après](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Apr%C3%A8s "L'Après") on 12 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive11-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-LMlive11) The five deputies ultimately announced that they would join the [Ecologist group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecologist_Group "Ecologist Group") on 15 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive13-1)[\[276\]](#refnote-LMlive13)

The [Democratic and Republican Left group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_and_Republican_Left_group "Democratic and Republican Left group") appeared on course to meet the threshold for 15 deputies in the new National Assembly, with Chassaigne counting 8 PCF deputies, 1 [Republican and Socialist Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_and_Socialist_Left "Republican and Socialist Left") (GRS) deputy, and "8 or 9" deputies from [overseas France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France "Overseas France").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-4)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8)

Of the 22 deputies in the [Liberties, Independents, Overseas and Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberties,_Independents,_Overseas_and_Territories "Liberties, Independents, Overseas and Territories") (LIOT) group before the snap election was called, 14 were re-elected. Because the group is so close to the threshold of 15 deputies needed, negotiations are likely to make the continuation of the group possible in the new National Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-335) However, [Martine Froger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martine_Froger "Martine Froger") and [David Taupiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Taupiac "David Taupiac"), re-elected with the support of the NFP, declined to comment on the subject, as was the case with [Paul Molac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Molac "Paul Molac"), [Jean-Luc Warsmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Luc_Warsmann "Jean-Luc Warsmann"), and [Estelle Youssouffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estelle_Youssouffa "Estelle Youssouffa"), while [Yannick Favennec-Bécot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannick_Favennec-B%C3%A9cot "Yannick Favennec-Bécot") was noncommittal. [Michel Castellani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Castellani "Michel Castellani"), [Paul-André Colombani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul-Andr%C3%A9_Colombani "Paul-André Colombani"), [Charles de Courson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Courson "Charles de Courson"), [Stéphane Lenormand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Lenormand "Stéphane Lenormand"), [Max Mathiasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Mathiasin "Max Mathiasin"), [Christophe Naegelen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christophe_Naegelen "Christophe Naegelen"), and [Olivier Serva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Serva "Olivier Serva") are all committed to re-forming the group, while [Valérie Létard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val%C3%A9rie_L%C3%A9tard "Valérie Létard") expressed her desire to assert her "independence" as her predecessor [Béatrice Descamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A9atrice_Descamps "Béatrice Descamps") did in the LIOT group.[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-336) Lenormand was elected president of the group of exactly 15 deputies on 16 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LPlive-2)[\[265\]](#refnote-LPlive)

The Elysée stated that Macron would wait for the new National Assembly to organise itself before making "the necessary decisions."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-8)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7) On 12 July, Attal announced that he would be a candidate for the presidency of the [Renaissance group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_group "Renaissance group") and that it would be renamed to the "Together for the Republic group" ( _groupe Ensemble pour la République_) as well,[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-337) and was elected on 13 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive12-1)[\[275\]](#refnote-LMlive12)

Renaissance deputy [Sacha Houlié](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacha_Houli%C3%A9 "Sacha Houlié"), representing the left flank of his party, pleaded with his colleagues to form a new "social democratic" group in the National Assembly,[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-338) and was able to quickly gather 20 deputies for his initiative. With many elected deputies embittered with Macron, only 33 deputies committed to joining the [Renaissance group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_group "Renaissance group"), with others preferring to maintain their leverage and threatening to leave for Houlié's group or join the MoDem in the event of an alliance with the right,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Renaissancegroup-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-Renaissancegroup) and Houlié officially announced on 10 July that he would not join the Renaissance group, which 40 deputies elected under the banner of Renaissance had yet to join, and instead form his own "ranging from the social right to the socialist left," saying that he didn't "agree with \[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-6)[the pro-Macron line\] anymore," adding that he felt closest to the views of Glucksmann.[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9) His efforts were ultimately unsuccessful, with 97 of 98 Renaissance deputies joining the group.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive12-2)[\[275\]](#refnote-LMlive12)

The [Horizons group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizons_group_(National_Assembly) "Horizons group (National Assembly)") will be re-constituted in the new National Assembly,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Renaissancegroup-1)[\[302\]](#refnote-Renaissancegroup) as will the [MoDem group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic,_MoDem_and_Independents_group "Democratic, MoDem and Independents group") to be headed by [Marc Fesneau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Fesneau "Marc Fesneau") after being elected president on 10 July against [Nicolas Turquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Turquois "Nicolas Turquois") and previous group president [Jean-Paul Mattei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Mattei "Jean-Paul Mattei"), having already led the group between June 2017 and November 2018. However, as a minister in the current government, he must choose to resign from either the government or as deputy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-340)

On 9 July, [Aurélien Pradié](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aur%C3%A9lien_Pradi%C3%A9 "Aurélien Pradié") stated that he believed there would no longer be a [LR group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_group_(National_Assembly) "The Republicans group (National Assembly)") within the National Assembly, expecting a change in both their name and way of operation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-5)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8) On 10 July, Wauquiez, elected as leader of the group upon his return to the National Assembly, announced that the group would change its name to become "The Republican Right" ( _La Droite Républicaine_).[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive9-7)[\[263\]](#refnote-LMlive9)

[Éric Ciotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89ric_Ciotti "Éric Ciotti") announced the creation of a new group in the National Assembly called [On the Right!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Right!_group "On the Right! group") ( _À droite!_), with 15 of 17 LR deputies elected with the support of the RN appearing in a photo posted on social media.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive8-6)[\[268\]](#refnote-LMlive8)

Bardella also implied that deputies elected with the support of the RN who were implicated in various controversies during the campaign would not sit with the [National Rally group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_group_(National_Assembly) "National Rally group (National Assembly)") in the legislature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive7-9)[\[247\]](#refnote-LMlive7)

## Notes

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2024_French_legislative_election&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Notes")\]

01. [&#8202;](#refnote-1)[↑](#ref-back-1-0) "Jump up")Seat number of predecessor alliance [New Ecological and Social People's Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Ecological_and_Social_People's_Union "New Ecological and Social People's Union") (NUPES), corresponding to candidate classifications of the [Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Interior_(France) "Minister of the Interior (France)")
02. [&#8202;](#refnote-NFPtotal)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-NFPtotal-0) [2](#ref-back-NFPtotal-1) [3](#ref-back-NFPtotal-2) [4](#ref-back-NFPtotal-3) [5](#ref-back-NFPtotal-4) [6](#ref-back-NFPtotal-5) [7](#ref-back-NFPtotal-6) [8](#ref-back-NFPtotal-7) [9](#ref-back-NFPtotal-8) [10](#ref-back-NFPtotal-9) [11](#ref-back-NFPtotal-10) [12](#ref-back-NFPtotal-11)Vote and seat calculations include the following codes assigned by the Ministry of the Interior, corresponding to the alliance or its components: UG, FI, SOC, VEC, and COM. The Ministry of Interior did not report fully disaggregated results by alliance member, with the latter 4 codes only assigned to 11 candidates in total, largely running in either [Corsica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsica "Corsica") or [overseas France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France "Overseas France"), of which 7 are categorised by _Le Monde_ as candidates of the New Popular Front. Because the official candidate classifications of the Ministry of Interior and unofficial classifications of _Le Monde_ and other sources may differ due to media organisations creating their own candidate classifications, the vote and seat totals reported here may differ from those in other sources. According to _Le Monde'_ s analysis, 182 NFP-affiliated candidates were elected. The seat total here reflects the inclusion of two candidates elected under the SOC code associated with the PS, but is not inclusive of the generic ECO code attributed to miscellaneous ecologist candidates including [Delphine Batho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphine_Batho "Delphine Batho") who was officially supported by the New Popular Front, and both this tally and those compiled by other sources may not fully account for all regionalist (REG) or miscellaneous left (DVG) candidates who claim to have the NFP's support, particularly those in [overseas France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France "Overseas France").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-9)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-12)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-7)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6)
03. [&#8202;](#refnote-Etotal)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-Etotal-0) [2](#ref-back-Etotal-1) [3](#ref-back-Etotal-2) [4](#ref-back-Etotal-3) [5](#ref-back-Etotal-4) [6](#ref-back-Etotal-5) [7](#ref-back-Etotal-6) [8](#ref-back-Etotal-7) [9](#ref-back-Etotal-8) [10](#ref-back-Etotal-9) [11](#ref-back-Etotal-10) [12](#ref-back-Etotal-11)Vote and seat calculations include the following codes assigned by the Ministry of the Interior, corresponding to the alliance or its components: ENS, REN, MDM, HOR, and UDI; the latter two codes contain 48 candidates, of which 23 are classified by _Le Monde_ as being Ensemble candidates. The Ministry of Interior did not report fully disaggregated results by alliance member, and the codes REN and MDM are not applied to any candidates. Because the official candidate classifications of the Ministry of Interior and unofficial classifications of _Le Monde_ and other sources may differ due to media organisations creating their own candidate classifications, the vote and seat totals reported here may differ from those in other sources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-10)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-13)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024) According to _Le Monde'_ s analysis, 168 Ensemble-affiliated candidates were elected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-8)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6)
04. [&#8202;](#refnote-LR)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-LR-0) [2](#ref-back-LR-1)[Éric Ciotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89ric_Ciotti "Éric Ciotti") was _de jure_ [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") (LR) party leader (after being restored in the interim by a Paris court) but presented a rival set of around 60 candidates in an alliance with the [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally") (RN) against roughly 400 candidates selected by the national investiture committee of The Republicans ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _commission nationale d'investiture des Républicains_) under [Annie Genevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Genevard "Annie Genevard"), [François-Xavier Bellamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois-Xavier_Bellamy "François-Xavier Bellamy"), and [Daniel Fasquelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Fasquelle "Daniel Fasquelle"), who were installed as interim party presidents before Ciotti's reinstatement as president and party member.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-95)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNI-2)[\[72\]](#refnote-CNI)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNILR-1)[\[74\]](#refnote-CNILR)[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-97)
05. [&#8202;](#refnote-7)[↑](#ref-back-7-0) "Jump up")Includes elected candidates jointly invested by the Ciotti-led faction of the LR and backed by the RN ( [Union of the Far-Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Far-Right "Union of the Far-Right"), UXD), which won 17 out of total 142 seats won by RN-led alliance. One other candidate ( [Eddy Casterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddy_Casterman "Eddy Casterman"), officially assigned to the miscellaneous far-right code EXD) was elected with the support of the RN but is neither a member of the party nor classified by the Ministry of the Interior as an RN candidate, for a total of 143 reported in some media sources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-11)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-results2024-14)[\[2\]](#refnote-results2024)
06. [&#8202;](#refnote-CNI)[↑](#ref-back-CNI-0) "Jump up")Elected candidates presented by the national investiture commission of The Republicans ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _Commission nationale d'investiture des Républicains_) only; this figure is disputed in some sources due to the classification of numerous candidates who have distanced themselves from LR as [miscellaneous right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscellaneous_right "Miscellaneous right") even if they are still members of the party. The party officially claims to have elected 56 candidates, while _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_ classifies 45 elected deputies as LR candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LMlive6-9)[\[3\]](#refnote-LMlive6)
07. [&#8202;](#refnote-10)[↑](#ref-back-10-0) "Jump up")In France, the Prime Minister is never elected but appointed by the President of the Republic. Only afterwards can the National Assembly chose to launch a motion of no-confidence against an the appointed government.
08. [&#8202;](#refnote-72)[↑](#ref-back-72-0) "Jump up")The party has also been described as [centre-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics "Centre-left politics"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-55) [radical centrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_centrist "Radical centrist"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-62) [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_wing_in_France "Right wing in France"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-67) or a [big tent/catch-all party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent "Big tent").[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-71)
09. [&#8202;](#refnote-74)[↑](#ref-back-74-0) "Jump up")Excluding [UDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democrats_and_Independents "Union of Democrats and Independents"), who announced their participation in the alliance for the legislative elections on 13 June 2024[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-73)
10. [&#8202;](#refnote-75)[↑](#ref-back-75-0) "Jump up")Includes 78 candidates, also presenting 1 candidate against official Ensemble candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-12)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
11. [&#8202;](#refnote-76)[↑](#ref-back-76-0) "Jump up")Includes 77 candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-13)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
12. [&#8202;](#refnote-77)[↑](#ref-back-77-0) "Jump up")Includes 3 candidates, also presenting 1 candidate against official Ensemble candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-14)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
13. [&#8202;](#refnote-78)[↑](#ref-back-78-0) "Jump up")Includes 11 candidates, also presenting 27 candidates against official Ensemble candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-15)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
14. [&#8202;](#refnote-80)[↑](#ref-back-80-0) "Jump up")Also includes one candidate each for the [New Anticapitalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Anticapitalist_Party "New Anticapitalist Party") (29 candidates invested outside of the framework of the NFP) and _[abertzale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abertzale "Abertzale")_ ( [EH Bai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euskal_Herria_Bai "Euskal Herria Bai")), as well as several other candidates not part of any political party or affiliated with smaller political formations[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-16)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHNFP-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-LHNFP)
15. [&#8202;](#refnote-NPF1)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-NPF1-0) [2](#ref-back-NPF1-1)Also presenting 1 candidate against official New Popular Front candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-17)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
16. [&#8202;](#refnote-84)[↑](#ref-back-84-0) "Jump up")Includes five candidates for [Ecological Revolution for the Living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_Revolution_for_the_Living "Ecological Revolution for the Living") ( [Aymeric Caron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aymeric_Caron "Aymeric Caron"), Laura Vallée-Hans, Victor Prandt, Jade Beniguel, Leslie Mortreux);[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-82) two candidates each for [Péyi-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9yi-A "Péyi-A") (excluding one attached candidate and one candidate invested outside the framework of the NFP) and [Rézistans Égalité 974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9zistans_%C3%89galit%C3%A9_974 "Rézistans Égalité 974"); and one candidate each for [Picardie Debout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picardie_Debout "Picardie Debout"), the [Independent Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Workers'_Party "Independent Workers' Party"), and the [Ecosocialist Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosocialist_Left "Ecosocialist Left")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reunion-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-Reunion)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-18)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHNFP-1)[\[66\]](#refnote-LHNFP)
17. [&#8202;](#refnote-85)[↑](#ref-back-85-0) "Jump up")Also presenting 6 candidates against official New Popular Front candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-19)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
18. [&#8202;](#refnote-87)[↑](#ref-back-87-0) "Jump up")Includes eight candidates for [Place Publique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Publique "Place Publique") (Pascaline Lécorché, Alain Roubian, [Aurélien Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aur%C3%A9lien_Rousseau "Aurélien Rousseau"), Yves Trousselle, Raphaël Pitti, Théa Fourdrinier, Sarah Breffy, and Guillaume Sacriste)[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-86) and one candidate each for [Le Progrès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Progr%C3%A8s_(R%C3%A9union) "Le Progrès (Réunion)") and the [Progressive Democratic Party of Guadeloupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Democratic_Party_of_Guadeloupe "Progressive Democratic Party of Guadeloupe")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reunion-1)[\[68\]](#refnote-Reunion)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-20)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
19. [&#8202;](#refnote-88)[↑](#ref-back-88-0) "Jump up")Includes 12 candidates from [Génération.s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9n%C3%A9ration.s "Génération.s") and one candidate from [Ecology Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecology_Generation "Ecology Generation")[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHNFP-2)[\[66\]](#refnote-LHNFP)
20. [&#8202;](#refnote-89)[↑](#ref-back-89-0) "Jump up")Includes three candidates of [For Réunion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_R%C3%A9union_(political_party) "For Réunion (political party)") (PLR): [Karine Lebon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karine_Lebon "Karine Lebon"), Alexis Chaussalet, and [Frédéric Maillot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Maillot "Frédéric Maillot");[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reunion-2)[\[68\]](#refnote-Reunion) two candidates for [Tāvini Huiraʻatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81vini_Huira%CA%BBatira "Tāvini Huiraʻatira") (one candidate invested outside the framework of the New Popular Front); and one candidate for the [Republican and Socialist Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_and_Socialist_Left "Republican and Socialist Left") (one candidate invested outside the framework of the New Popular Front)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-21)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LHNFP-3)[\[66\]](#refnote-LHNFP)
21. [&#8202;](#refnote-92)[↑](#ref-back-92-0) "Jump up")Includes candidates jointly backed by the Ciotti-led [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") faction. The right-wing populist party [Debout la France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debout_la_France "Debout la France") will present its own candidates in only 76 constituencies as classified by _Le Monde_ (although the party officially claims to have 107 candidates), including against RN candidates, and will simultaneously support RN-backed candidates in other constituencies[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-22)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DLF1-1)[\[70\]](#refnote-DLF1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DLF2-1)[\[71\]](#refnote-DLF2)
22. [&#8202;](#refnote-seattotal)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-seattotal-0) [2](#ref-back-seattotal-1)Seat totals account for the [Ciotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89ric_Ciotti "Éric Ciotti")–RN alliance ( [UXD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Far-Right "Union of the Far-Right")) and totals attributed to LR are solely for candidates presented by the national investiture committee of The Republicans ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _commission nationale d'investiture des Républicains_). Current totals account for Ciotti and [Christelle d'Intorni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christelle_d'Intorni "Christelle d'Intorni") running as candidates in their constituencies in alliance with the RN without the endorsement of the national investiture committee of The Republicans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNI-3)[\[72\]](#refnote-CNI)
23. [&#8202;](#refnote-99)[↑](#ref-back-99-0) "Jump up")Candidates invested in 329 (57.0%) constituencies according to the classification of _Le Monde_, although the party officially claims to have invested candidates in 330 (57.2%) constituencies[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-23)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rec1-1)[\[76\]](#refnote-Rec1)
24. [&#8202;](#refnote-100)[↑](#ref-back-100-0) "Jump up")Candidates invested in 550 (95.3%) constituencies[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-24)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
25. [&#8202;](#refnote-UXD)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-UXD-0) [2](#ref-back-UXD-1) [3](#ref-back-UXD-2) [4](#ref-back-UXD-3)The designation " [union of the far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_far-right "Union of the far-right")" ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _union de l'extrême droite_, UXD) is used by the Ministry of the Interior to refer to candidates jointly invested by the Ciotti-led faction of [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") and supported by the [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally").[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-255)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-25)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
26. [&#8202;](#refnote-LRtotal)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-LRtotal-0) [2](#ref-back-LRtotal-1) [3](#ref-back-LRtotal-2) [4](#ref-back-LRtotal-3)Vote and seat totals corresponding to LR candidates invested by the national investiture committee of The Republicans ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _commission nationale d'investiture des Républicains_), including some directly in opposition to Ciotti himself and other candidates presented by his alliance with the RN in certain constituencies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNI-4)[\[72\]](#refnote-CNI)
27. [&#8202;](#refnote-258)[↑](#ref-back-258-0) "Jump up")Includes 37 LR candidates, 5 Ensemble candidates, 3 union of the far-right candidates, 2 dissident LR candidates, 1 non-Ensemble MoDem candidate, 1 non-Ensemble UDI candidate, and 1 dissident Ensemble candidate out of 190 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-26)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
28. [&#8202;](#refnote-259)[↑](#ref-back-259-0) "Jump up")Includes 9 NFP candidates, 1 Ensemble candidate, 11 dissident NFP candidates, and 1 non-NFP candidate from a NFP member party out of 140 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-27)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
29. [&#8202;](#refnote-260)[↑](#ref-back-260-0) "Jump up")Includes 8 Ensemble candidates, 13 non-Ensemble [Union of Democrats and Independents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democrats_and_Independents "Union of Democrats and Independents") candidates of 38 total UDI candidates, 5 of 7 [Les Centristes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Centristes "Les Centristes") candidates, 2 LR candidates, 1 non-Ensemble [Radical Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Party_(France) "Radical Party (France)") candidate, and 1 Ensemble dissident out of 149 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-28)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
30. [&#8202;](#refnote-261)[↑](#ref-back-261-0) "Jump up")Includes 549 of 550 [Lutte Ouvrière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutte_Ouvri%C3%A8re "Lutte Ouvrière") candidates, 29 of 30 [New Anticapitalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Anticapitalist_Party "New Anticapitalist Party") candidates, and 1 NFP dissident out of 654 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-29)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
31. [&#8202;](#refnote-262)[↑](#ref-back-262-0) "Jump up")Includes 14 [Unser Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unser_Land "Unser Land") candidates, 3 [Femu a Corsica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femu_a_Corsica "Femu a Corsica") candidates, 3 [Occitan Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_Party "Occitan Party") candidates, 3 of 13 [Résistons!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9sistons_! "Résistons !") candidates, 3 [Tāvini Huiraʻatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81vini_Huira%CA%BBatira "Tāvini Huiraʻatira") candidates (of which 2 are NFP candidates), 2 other NFP candidates, and 2 [Party of the Corsican Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Corsican_Nation "Party of the Corsican Nation") candidates out of 132 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-30)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
32. [&#8202;](#refnote-263)[↑](#ref-back-263-0) "Jump up")Includes 19 of 23 [Ecology at the Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecology_at_the_Centre "Ecology at the Centre") candidates, 2 non-Ensemble [Union of Democrats and Independents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democrats_and_Independents "Union of Democrats and Independents") candidates out of 38 total candidates, 1 non-NFP candidate from a NFP member party, and 1 NFP candidate out of 144 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-31)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
33. [&#8202;](#refnote-264)[↑](#ref-back-264-0) "Jump up")Includes 1 Ensemble candidate, 1 LR candidate, and 1 NFP dissident out of 215 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-32)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
34. [&#8202;](#refnote-265)[↑](#ref-back-265-0) "Jump up")Includes 74 of 76 [Debout la France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debout_la_France "Debout la France") candidates classified by _Le Monde_ (although the party officially claims to have 107 candidates) out of 114 total sovereignist right candidates, with DLF officially backing RN candidates in a majority of constituencies[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-33)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DLF1-2)[\[70\]](#refnote-DLF1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DLF2-2)[\[71\]](#refnote-DLF2)
35. [&#8202;](#refnote-266)[↑](#ref-back-266-0) "Jump up")Includes 3 RN and 2 Reconquête candidates out of 23 total candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-34)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
36. [&#8202;](#refnote-269)[↑](#ref-back-269-0) "Jump up")The Radical Party of the Left, abbreviated PRG (not to be confused with the Radicals of the Left, [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _les Radicaux de gauche_, abbreviated LRDG but not related to the code RDG assigned to the PRG), is not officially a member or supporter of the NFP, but supports a left-of-centre coalition and primarily left-of-centre candidates. This code corresponding to the PRG, RDG, has only been assigned to 4 candidates by the Ministry of the Interior, including 1 Ensemble candidate,[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-35)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-267) while one candidate, Kira Bacar Adacolo, is a member of the PRG and supported by the NFP but classified as a [miscellaneous left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscellaneous_left "Miscellaneous left") candidate by the Ministry of the Interior.[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-268)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LM1-36)[\[1\]](#refnote-LM1)
37. [&#8202;](#refnote-294)[↑](#ref-back-294-0) "Jump up")The vote shares and subgroup data in this analysis were weighted to match Ipsos's initial vote share estimates released at 20:00 CEST on the night of the first round, so do not precisely match the final results.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-293)
